In the paper "FUTURE-AI: Guiding Principles and Consensus Recommendations for Trustworthy Artificial Intelligence in Future Medical Imaging," a team of experts in the field of artificial intelligence (AI) and medical imaging present a set of guiding principles and consensus recommendations for the development and deployment of trustworthy AI in future medical imaging. The paper is the result of a collaborative effort between academia, industry, and regulatory bodies to address the ethical, social, and technical challenges associated with the use of AI in medical imaging. The authors argue that as AI becomes increasingly integrated into medical imaging, it is essential to ensure that these systems are not only technically proficient but also trustworthy, transparent, and ethical. They propose a set of guiding principles that emphasize the importance of patient-centered design, transparency, accountability, and inclusivity in the development and deployment of AI in medical imaging. The paper also presents consensus recommendations for the development, validation, and deployment of AI in medical imaging, including the need for diverse and representative datasets, robust testing and validation methodologies, and ongoing monitoring and evaluation of AI performance. The authors emphasize the importance of addressing the ethical and social implications of AI in medical imaging, such as ensuring that AI systems do not perpetuate or exacerbate existing health disparities. The authors also highlight the need for a collaborative approach to the development and regulation of AI in medical imaging, involving stakeholders from academia, industry, regulatory bodies, and patient advocacy groups. In this paper, we present EdgeFlow, a novel approach to interactive segmentation that leverages the concept of edge-guided flow to achieve practical and efficient segmentation of images. Interactive segmentation is a fundamental task in computer vision that involves allowing a user to interact with an image to indicate the regions of interest. However, existing methods for interactive segmentation are often limited by their reliance on iterative user input, which can be time-consuming and may not accurately capture the user's intended segmentation. EdgeFlow addresses these limitations by introducing a new approach that combines the efficiency of edge-guided segmentation with the practicality of interactive segmentation. Our approach uses a novel edge-guided flow algorithm that propagates user input along the edges of an image, allowing for fast and accurate segmentation of complex images. We demonstrate the effectiveness of EdgeFlow through extensive experiments on several benchmark datasets, showing that it outperforms state-of-the-art interactive segmentation methods in terms of both efficiency and accuracy. In addition, we explore the versatility of EdgeFlow by applying it to a variety of applications, including object segmentation, medical image segmentation, and video segmentation. Our results show that EdgeFlow can handle a wide range of scenarios, from simple object segmentation to complex medical image segmentation, and can accurately segment objects in real-time. Overall, EdgeFlow represents a significant advancement in the field of interactive segmentation, providing a practical and efficient approach that can be used in a variety of applications. In this paper, we present a novel approach to interactive segmentation called EdgeFlow, which leverages edge information to guide the segmentation process and achieve practical interaction efficiency. Existing methods often suffer from slow convergence or rely on heuristics that limit their accuracy, making them impractical for real-world applications. EdgeFlow addresses these limitations by introducing an edge-guided flow algorithm that combines the benefits of both boundary-following and gradient-descent-based methods. The proposed method efficiently explores the image space while adaptively adjusting its behavior based on local edge features, ensuring accurate segmentation and minimizing user input. We evaluate EdgeFlow on several benchmark datasets and compare it to state-of-the-art methods in terms of efficiency, accuracy, and usability. Our results show significant advantages in terms of both computational cost and user effort required to obtain high-quality segmentations. Additionally, we demonstrate the versatility of EdgeFlow through applications in various domains such as object recognition, medical imaging, and video analysis. In this paper, we propose a novel approach to medical image segmentation using semi-supervised contrastive learning. Our approach leverages the large amount of unlabelled data available in medical imaging to improve segmentation accuracy, while reducing the need for annotated data. We introduce a new framework that combines contrastive learning with a segmentation model, enabling the model to learn from both labelled and unlabelled data. The proposed method is evaluated on several medical image segmentation tasks, including brain tumor segmentation and lung segmentation, and shows superior performance compared to supervised learning methods that rely solely on annotated data. Our results demonstrate the potential of semi-supervised contrastive learning for label-efficient medical image segmentation, and highlight the importance of exploiting unlabelled data to improve segmentation accuracy.
In the field of medical image segmentation, imbalanced data can pose significant challenges, particularly when dealing with multi-organ segmentation tasks. The scarcity of certain organ instances or classes can result in biased models that perform poorly on unseen data. To address this issue, this paper proposes a novel loss function called Combo Loss, which is designed to handle input and output imbalance in multi-organ segmentation tasks. Combo Loss combines two main components: a class-balanced loss function and a spatially-aware loss function. The class-balanced loss function addresses output imbalance by up-weighting the minority classes, while the spatially-aware loss function addresses input imbalance by penalizing the model for incorrectly segmenting organs that are spatially close to the true positive instances. The authors evaluate Combo Loss on two publicly available datasets: the Medical Segmentation Decathlon dataset and the Cancer Genome Atlas (TCGA) dataset. The results show that Combo Loss outperforms state-of-the-art loss functions, including Dice loss and Lovász softmax, in terms of segmentation accuracy and robustness to input and output imbalance. Furthermore, the authors perform a series of ablation studies to analyze the effectiveness of the two components of Combo Loss. The results demonstrate that both components contribute to the improved performance, and that the combination of the two components leads to better results than using either component alone. In medical image segmentation, imbalanced input and output data can significantly affect the performance of deep learning models. This issue is particularly challenging in multi-organ segmentation tasks, where multiple organs with varying sizes, shapes, and locations need to be accurately segmented. To address this problem, we propose a novel loss function called Combo Loss that efficiently handles input and output imbalance in multi-organ segmentation. Combo Loss combines two main components: an asymmetric loss and a clustering loss. The asymmetric loss encourages the model to focus on the accurate segmentation of smaller organs while the clustering loss promotes spatial consistency among pixels belonging to the same organ. We evaluated our proposed method on several publicly available datasets, including the Medical ImageNet dataset, which contains various abdominal organs, and compared it against state-of-the-art methods. Our experiments demonstrate that Combo Loss outperforms existing loss functions in terms of both accuracy and efficiency. Specifically, it achieves a higher mean Intersection over Union (mIoU) score and requires fewer annotations. Moreover, we perform extensive ablation studies to analyze the effectiveness of each component of Combo Loss. These results provide valuable insights into how different design choices impact the overall performance of the loss function. Finally, we showcase the versatility of Combo Loss by applying it to other medical segmentation tasks such as brain structures segmentation from magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) scans. In this paper, we propose a novel approach for training an attention-based 3D seismic fault segmentation model using a limited number of 2D slice labels. Our approach leverages the power of deep learning to accurately identify and segment faults in 3D seismic data, while significantly reducing the amount of labeling effort required. Existing methods for 3D seismic fault segmentation typically require a large number of manually labeled 3D samples, which can be time-consuming and expensive to obtain. In contrast, our approach uses a few 2D slice labels to train a 3D attention-based model that can accurately segment faults in the entire 3D volume. The proposed method consists of two main components: a 2D labeling step and a 3D training step. In the 2D labeling step, we manually label a small number of 2D slices from the 3D seismic data to create a set of 2D fault labels. These labels are then used to train a 2D attention-based fault segmentation model. In the 3D training step, we use the trained 2D model to generate a set of 3D attention weights, which are then used to train a 3D attention-based fault segmentation model. We evaluate the performance of our approach using a dataset of 3D seismic data with manually labeled faults. Our results show that our approach can accurately segment faults in 3D seismic data with a high degree of accuracy, while significantly reducing the amount of labeling effort required. Existing approaches for 3D seismic fault segmentation typically require large amounts of manual annotation effort, which can be time-consuming and expensive. To address this challenge, we propose a weakly supervised learning framework that utilizes only a few labeled 2D slices to train a 3D segmentation model. The key idea is to use the scarce labels to guide the attention mechanism, which focuses on the most informative regions of the input data and improves the accuracy of the segmentation model. We evaluate our approach on several synthetic and real-world datasets, demonstrating its effectiveness in comparison with fully supervised models and other state-of-the-art methods. Our results show that the attention-based approach achieves competitive performance while requiring significantly fewer annotated samples. This suggests that the proposed method has great potential for reducing the labeling effort required for 3D seismic fault segmentation. In this paper, we present Segmenter, a novel approach to semantic segmentation that leverages the power of Transformers to accurately and efficiently segment images into their constituent parts. Semantic segmentation is a fundamental task in computer vision, with applications in a wide range of domains, including autonomous driving, medical imaging, and robotics. Existing approaches to semantic segmentation rely on convolutional neural networks (CNNs), which are limited in their ability to capture long-range dependencies and contextual information. In contrast, Transformers have proven highly effective in natural language processing tasks, where long-range dependencies are crucial. We hypothesize that Transformers can similarly improve performance in semantic segmentation tasks. Our Segmenter model replaces traditional CNNs with Transformers, using self-attention mechanisms to capture contextual information and long-range dependencies between image pixels. We evaluate Segmenter on several benchmark datasets, including Cityscapes, PASCAL VOC, and COCO, and show that it outperforms state-of-the-art CNN-based models in terms of both accuracy and efficiency. In addition to its improved performance, Segmenter has several advantages over traditional CNN-based models. It requires fewer parameters and computations, making it more computationally efficient and easier to train. It is also more scalable, allowing for larger input sizes and faster inference times. Our results demonstrate the effectiveness of Transformers in semantic segmentation tasks, and highlight the potential of Segmenter as a valuable tool for a wide range of applications. We further analyze the contributions of different components of the Segmenter model, providing insights into the role of self-attention and other design choices in its performance.

Traditional methods rely on fully convolutional networks (FCNs) that suffer from limited receptive field and inability to model long-range dependencies. Next, we outline the architecture of Segmenter, consisting of a multi-layer encoder and decoder with attention mechanisms. We also introduce a novel technique called Spatial Attention Masking (SAM), which allows the model to focus on relevant regions and reduce computational complexity. Our experimental results show that Segmenter outperforms FCNs on multiple datasets including Cityscapes, PASCAL VOC, and CamVid. Specifically, we achieve an increase of +4.3% mIoU on Cityscapes and +5.1% mIoU on CamVid compared to the baseline FCN model. Additionally, we provide extensive ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of SAM and other key components of Segmenter. In this paper, we propose a novel approach for semantic segmentation that goes beyond traditional image-based methods by incorporating contextual information from various sources. Semantic segmentation is a fundamental task in computer vision that aims to assign a semantic label to each pixel in an image. While state-of-the-art methods have achieved impressive results using deep learning techniques, they are limited by their reliance on image data alone. We argue that contextual information, such as textual descriptions, object categories, and scene information, can provide valuable cues for improving segmentation accuracy. Our approach, called Contextual Segmentation Network (CSN), leverages a multimodal fusion framework to integrate image and contextual information in a single neural network. The CSN model consists of three main components: an image encoder, a context encoder, and a fusion module. The image encoder extracts features from the input image using a convolutional neural network (CNN), while the context encoder processes the contextual information using a natural language processing (NLP) technique. The fusion module combines the image and context features using a novel attention mechanism, enabling the model to weigh the importance of different contextual cues for each pixel. We evaluate the CSN model on several benchmark datasets, including PASCAL VOC and Cityscapes, and demonstrate its superior performance compared to state-of-the-art image-based segmentation methods. Our experiments show that incorporating contextual information can improve segmentation accuracy by up to 10% in some cases, especially for objects with similar appearance or in scenes with complex context. In this paper, we propose a novel approach to semantic segmentation that leverages contextual information beyond the image itself. Traditional methods of semantic segmentation rely solely on the visual content of the image, neglecting valuable contextual information that can improve accuracy and robustness. We introduce a framework that incorporates external knowledge and priorities to enhance the feature representation and overcome the limitations of standard CNN-based segmentation models. Our approach consists of two main components: (1) a knowledge injection module that incorporates external knowledge from various sources, such as textual descriptions, object categories, and scene understanding, into the feature extraction process; and (2) a priority-aware module that adapts the feature representations based on the importance of each pixel in the image. We evaluate our method on several benchmark datasets, including Cityscapes, PASCAL VOC, and COCO, and demonstrate significant improvements over state-of-the-art segmentation methods. Our experimental results show that the proposed method outperforms state-of-the-art segmentation approaches, demonstrating the effectiveness of mining contextual information beyond the image for semantic segmentation tasks. Our proposed method enables users to interact with an image using natural language commands, gestures, and visual attention, allowing them to accurately refer to specific regions of interest within the image. We propose a unified framework that combines these multiple modalities into a single coherent representation, enabling effective communication and collaboration between humans and machines. In this paper, we propose a novel approach to few-shot segmentation, which is a challenging task in computer vision that involves segmenting objects from a small number of labeled examples. Our approach, called Few-Shot Segmentation with Global and Local Contrastive Learning (FSS-GCL), combines the strengths of global and local contrastive learning to improve segmentation accuracy. Global contrastive learning has been shown to be effective in learning high-level representations that are useful for few-shot learning. However, it may not capture local information that is crucial for segmentation tasks. Local contrastive learning, on the other hand, can capture local information but may not generalize well to new examples. In FSS-GCL, we propose a two-stage training strategy that first uses global contrastive learning to learn high-level representations and then uses local contrastive learning to refine the segmentation masks. We also introduce a novel mask-based contrastive loss function that encourages the model to produce accurate segmentation masks. Experiments on several benchmark datasets show that FSS-GCL outperforms state-of-the-art few-shot segmentation methods, including those that use global or local contrastive learning alone. We also perform ablation studies to analyze the effectiveness of the different components of our approach. Our main contributions are: * A novel few-shot segmentation approach called FSS-GCL that combines global and local contrastive learning * A two-stage training strategy that leverages the strengths of global and local contrastive learning * A mask-based contrastive loss function that encourages accurate segmentation masks * State-of-the-art performance on several benchmark datasets Overall, FSS-GCL demonstrates the effectiveness of
In this paper, we present a novel approach to few-shot segmentation, which leverages both global and local contrastive learning to achieve state-of-the-art performance. Our proposed method, called FSGL (Few-Shot Segmentation with Global and Local Contrastive Learning), combines the strengths of global contrastive learning and local contrastive learning to improve the accuracy and efficiency of few-shot segmentation. Global contrastive learning is used to learn high-level features that are robust across different datasets and tasks, while local contrastive learning is employed to learn task-specific features that are important for segmenting specific objects or regions in an image. By combining these two types of contrastive learning, our model can learn both generalizable and task-specific knowledge, leading to improved performance on few-shot segmentation tasks. FSGL consists of two main components: a global feature extractor and a local feature refiner. The global feature extractor uses a self-supervised loss function to learn high-level features from a large dataset, while the local feature refiner uses a supervised loss function to fine-tune the learned features for a specific segmentation task. We also propose a novel technique called multi-scale dense connections to integrate the global and local features effectively. Experiments conducted on several benchmark datasets show that FSGL outperforms existing state-of-the-art few-shot segmentation methods by a significant margin. Notably, FSGL achieves an average IoU score of 86.5% on PASCAL VOC, which is 10.2% higher than the previous state-of-the-art method. In this paper, we propose a novel approach to constructing prediction sets that do not rely on any distributional assumptions and simultaneously control the risk of over-prediction. Our method, called distribution-free, risk-controlling prediction sets (DFRCPS), is based on a non-parametric, data-driven approach that leverages the concept of conformal prediction. We demonstrate that DFRCPS can be used to construct prediction sets with a guaranteed level of confidence, while also providing a risk-controlling mechanism to prevent over-prediction. We evaluate the performance of DFRCPS through simulations and real-world case studies, comparing it to existing methods such as conformal prediction and Bayesian prediction. Our results show that DFRCPS outperforms these methods in terms of both accuracy and risk control, particularly in situations where the underlying distribution is complex or unknown. The key contribution of this paper is the development of a distribution-free, risk-controlling approach to prediction set construction that does not require any distributional assumptions. Our method provides a principled way to balance the trade-off between accuracy and risk control, and can be applied to a wide range of prediction problems, including classification, regression, and time-series forecasting. In Section 4, we present the theoretical properties of DFRCPS, including its risk control and confidence guarantees. Abstract: This paper presents a novel approach to synthetic aperture sonar (SAS) image segmentation based on iterative, deep, and unsupervised learning techniques. SAS imaging is widely used in underwater exploration and surveillance, but its low-quality images pose significant challenges for accurate target detection and segmentation. Existing methods rely heavily on manual annotation and domain-specific feature extraction, which are time-consuming, tedious, and suboptimal. To address these limitations, we propose an iterative, deep, and unsupervised method that leverages recent advances in convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and clustering algorithms. Our approach consists of two main stages: (1) preliminary feature extraction using a CNN; and (2) iterative clustering and refinement enabled by a self-organizing map (SOM). The CNN extracts high-level features from the input SAS images, while the SOM grouping fosters spatial coherence and facilitates the formation of meaningful clusters. An iterative mechanism integrates the CNN and SOM outputs, allowing the algorithm to adaptively refine its segmentations as more data becomes available. Our exhaustive experiments on various real-world datasets demonstrate the superiority of our proposed method over traditional approaches, achieving an average precision increase of 15% and a mean IoU improvement of 0.3. Additionally, we provide thorough analyses of parameter sensitivity and performance consistency across different scenarios, offering insights into the strengths and limitations of deep, unsupervised SAS image segmentation. The contributions of this work can be summarized as follows: * Novel use of CNNs and SOMs for joint feature extraction and cluster identification in SAS image segmentation * Introduction of an iterative mechanism

Our proposed method, called Segmentation in Style, leverages the strengths of both models to generate high-quality segmentation masks for images without requiring any annotated data. StyleGAN, a generative adversarial network, is used to generate diverse styles for a given input image, while CLIP, a contrastive language-image model, is employed to learn a joint representation of images and their corresponding captions. By combining these two models, we can learn a rich set of features that capture both the visual and semantic information of the input images. We propose a novel loss function that combines the image-image and image-text contrastive losses to optimize the segmentation process. The image-image loss encourages the model to produce segmentation masks that are consistent across different styles of the same image, while the image-text loss encourages the model to produce masks that are semantically consistent with the corresponding captions. We evaluate our method on several benchmark datasets, including PASCAL VOC and COCO, and demonstrate superior performance compared to state-of-the-art unsupervised segmentation methods. Our experiments show that Segmentation in Style produces high-quality segmentation masks that accurately capture the object boundaries and semantics of the input images. Furthermore, we perform a series of ablation studies to analyze the effectiveness of different components of our method. In this paper, we propose a novel approach for semantic image segmentation called Double Similarity Distillation (DSD). Semantic image segmentation is a fundamental task in computer vision that involves assigning a class label to each pixel in an image, where the classes represent meaningful objects or regions. Existing methods for semantic image segmentation rely heavily on deep learning techniques, such as convolutional neural networks (CNNs), which can be computationally expensive and require large amounts of labeled training data. In contrast, our proposed method, DSD, leverages the power of similarity learning to improve the efficiency and accuracy of semantic image segmentation. Similarity learning is a technique that learns a similarity metric between images, which can be used for various tasks such as image retrieval, duplicate removal, and object recognition. In the context of semantic image segmentation, similarity learning can be used to identify pixels that belong to the same object or region. The key idea behind DSD is to distill the similarity knowledge from a pre-trained teacher model into a smaller student model. The teacher model is trained on a large dataset of labeled images, while the student model is trained on a smaller dataset of unlabeled images. By distilling the similarity knowledge from the teacher model, the student model can learn to recognize objects and regions in the image without requiring explicit labels. We evaluate the performance of DSD on several benchmark datasets, including PASCAL VOC and Cityscapes. Our results show that DSD outperforms state-of-the-art segmentation methods, including fully supervised
In this paper, we present a novel approach to semantic image segmentation called Double Similarity Distillation (DSD). This method leverages the power of similarity learning to improve the accuracy and efficiency of semantic segmentation models. Existing methods for semantic segmentation often rely on complex neural network architectures that require large amounts of annotated data and computational resources to train. In contrast, our proposed method uses a simple and efficient framework that exploits the concept of similarity between images. We demonstrate that by distilling the similarity information from two different levels of abstraction, we can significantly improve the performance of a semantic segmentation model. The key insight behind DSD is that similarities between images can be used to transfer knowledge across different domains and tasks. Specifically, we use a pre-trained convolutional neural network (CNN) to extract features from pairs of images that are semantically similar but visually distinct. These feature representations are then distilled into a lower-dimensional space using a contrastive loss function, which encourages the model to focus on the most discriminative aspects of the input data. We evaluate the effectiveness of DSD on several benchmark datasets, including PASCAL VOC and Cityscapes. Our results show that with minimal annotation effort and computation cost, DSD achieves competitive performance compared to state-of-the-art segmentation methods. In this paper, we propose a novel approach to medical image segmentation using a spatial guided self-supervised clustering network (SG-SCN). The SG-SCN model combines the strengths of self-supervised learning and spatial guidance to improve the accuracy and efficiency of medical image segmentation. Self-supervised learning has emerged as a promising approach to medical image segmentation, as it can learn useful features from unlabelled data. However, self-supervised learning methods often suffer from the problem of trivial solutions, where the model learns irrelevant or redundant features that do not generalize well to new data. Spatial guidance, which incorporates prior knowledge about the spatial structure of the data, has been shown to improve the performance of self-supervised learning models. The SG-SCN model proposed in this paper combines self-supervised learning with spatial guidance to overcome the limitations of traditional self-supervised learning methods. The model consists of a spatial guidance module that incorporates prior knowledge about the spatial structure of the data, and a self-supervised clustering module that learns to cluster the data into meaningful segments. The spatial guidance module is designed to provide the self-supervised clustering module with spatial information, such as the location and size of organs or tissues, which helps the model to learn more accurate and meaningful features. We evaluate the performance of the SG-SCN model on several publicly available medical image datasets, including brain MRI, lung CT, and liver CT. The results show that the SG-SCN model outperforms several state-of-the-art self-supervised learning methods, and achieves competitive performance compared to fully supervised

First, we train an FCN to learn high-level features from a large dataset of labeled medical images. Then, we use these features as input to a PGM, which models the spatial relationships between pixels and enforces label consistency across neighboring regions. The PGM is trained using a variety of datasets with different modalities and diseases, allowing us to learn a robust representation of the underlying anatomy. To improve our model's ability to handle variations in image quality and artifacts, we incorporate uncertainty estimates into the segmentation process. Specifically, we use dropout regularization to induce aleatoric uncertainty during training, and combine it with epistemic uncertainty from the PGM's predictions. This allows us to generate high-quality segmentations even when dealing with noisy or low-resolution inputs. We evaluate our method on several benchmark datasets encompassing diverse medical imaging modalities, including CT, MRI, and US. The results demonstrate that our approach outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of both accuracy and robustness. In this paper, we propose a novel semi-supervised learning approach for left atrium segmentation, which leverages mutual consistency training to improve segmentation accuracy. Our experiments show that CHASE outperforms several state-of-the-art tracking methods, including deep learning-based methods, and achieves a high success rate in various challenging scenarios. In this paper, we present CHASE (Robust Visual Tracking via Cell-Level Differentiable Neural Architecture Search), a novel approach to visual object tracking that leverages the power of differentiable neural architecture search (NAS) to optimize deep neural networks for robust tracking performance. Unlike traditional NAS methods that rely on coarse-grained searches over predefined architectures, CHASE performs cell-level differentiable search, allowing it to explore a vast space of possible network architectures and identify optimal designs tailored specifically to each target object. CHASE is built upon a recently proposed differentiable rendering engine, which enables efficient and accurate rendering of synthetic images that mimic real-world scenarios. By combining this capability with advanced optimization techniques and a carefully designed loss function, CHASE is able to learn high-performance trackers in an end-to-end manner, without requiring manual feature engineering or domain knowledge. Extensive experiments conducted on several challenging benchmark datasets demonstrate the superiority of CHASE compared to state-of-the-art tracking methods, including those that rely on handcrafted features or require elaborate postprocessing steps. Additionally, sensitivity analysis reveals that CHASE is highly robust to variations in input quality, pose, illumination, and occlusion, making it a promising solution for real-world applications where visual tracking systems must operate under diverse conditions. The main contributions of this work can be summarized as follows: * We introduce CHASE, a novel method for robotic visual tracking using cell-level differentiable neural architecture search. * CHASE conducts a comprehensive search over the space of possible network architectures, leading to optimized trackers tailored to individual objects. *
In this paper, we propose a novel approach to weakly supervised segmentation, called Inter Extreme Points Geodesics (IEPG), which leverages the concept of geodesics in the space of probability distributions to segment objects in images. Unlike traditional segmentation methods that rely on strongly supervised learning or fully convolutional networks, IEPG only requires weak supervision in the form of bounding boxes around the objects of interest. The key insight behind IEPG is that the geodesic distance between the probability distributions of pixels in the image and the bounding box can be used to identify the pixels that belong to the object. We propose an efficient algorithm to compute the geodesic distance and use it to optimize a segmentation mask that separates the object from the background. We evaluate the performance of IEPG on several benchmark datasets, including PASCAL VOC and Cityscapes, and show that it achieves competitive segmentation accuracy compared to state-of-the-art methods, while requiring significantly less annotated data. We also demonstrate the versatility of IEPG by applying it to various segmentation tasks, such as instance segmentation, semantic segmentation, and video segmentation. In this paper, we propose a novel approach to weakly supervised segmentation called Inter Extreme Points Geodesics (IEPG). This method leverages the concept of extreme points, which are points in the image that have the highest similarity to the boundary of the object of interest, and geodesic distances, which provide a metric for comparing the similarity between pixels. The IEPG algorithm consists of two main steps: 1) extracting extreme points from the image using a modified Canny edge detector, and 2) computing the geodesic distance matrix between all pairs of extreme points. The resulting matrix is then used to construct a graph, where each node represents an extreme point and edges connect points with similar geodesic distances. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach on several benchmark datasets, including PASCAL VOC and Cityscapes. Our experiments show that IEPG outperforms traditional weakly supervised segmentation methods, such as random walker and GCUT, in terms of both accuracy and efficiency. Additionally, we provide a comprehensive analysis of the impact of different parameters on the performance of IEPG, providing insights into how to optimize its use in practice. Our work has important implications for applications where obtaining large amounts of annotated data is difficult or expensive, such as medical imaging or satellite imagery. By leveraging weak annotations, IEPG enables accurate segmentation without requiring a large amount of manual annotation effort.

In this paper, we propose a novel approach to image segmentation called ComBiNet, which stands for Compact Convolutional Bayesian Neural Network. ComBiNet combines the strengths of convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and Bayesian neural networks (BNNs) to provide a compact and accurate solution for image segmentation tasks. The proposed ComBiNet model consists of a compact CNN architecture that leverages the power of depthwise separable convolutions and channel shuffle operations to reduce the number of parameters and computation required. The CNN is followed by a Bayesian layer that incorporates a spatial pyramid pooling module to capture multi-scale contextual information. The Bayesian layer is trained using a variational inference method, which enables the network to learn the posterior distribution over the class labels and produce uncertainty estimates for the segmentation masks. We evaluate the performance of ComBiNet on several benchmark datasets, including Cityscapes, PASCAL VOC, and COCO. Our experiments show that ComBiNet achieves state-of-the-art performance on these datasets while requiring significantly fewer parameters and computations compared to other state-of-the-art methods. We also perform a series of ablation studies to analyze the effectiveness of the key components of ComBiNet and demonstrate its superiority over traditional CNN and BNN models. The main contributions of this paper can be summarized as follows: * We propose a novel Compact Convolutional Bayesian Neural Network (ComBiNet) for image segmentation that combines the strengths of CNNs and BNNs. * We introduce a compact CNN architecture that leverages depthwise separable convolutions and channel shuffle operations to reduce the number of parameters

The paper "A Thorough View of Exact Inference in Graphs from the Degree-4 Sum-of-Squares Hierarchy" presents a comprehensive analysis of exact inference in graphs using the degree-4 sum-of-squares (SOS) hierarchy. This work builds upon previous research that established the SOS hierarchy as a powerful tool for solving graphical models, and provides a thorough understanding of its capabilities and limitations. The authors demonstrate that the degree-4 SOS hierarchy can be used to perform exact inference in various classes of graphs, including bounded-degree graphs, planar graphs, andgraphs with excluded minors. They provide explicit bounds on the computational complexity of exact inference using the SOS hierarchy for these classes of graphs, and show that it can be performed in polynomial time for many interesting cases. The paper also explores the connections between the SOS hierarchy and other approaches to exact inference, such as belief propagation and linear programming relaxations. The authors show that the SOS hierarchy can be seen as an alternate formulation of these methods, providing new insights into their strengths and weaknesses. Finally, the authors investigate the relationship between the SOS hierarchy and the statistical properties of graphs. They prove lower bounds on the size of the smallest witnesses required by the SOS hierarchy to refute independence claims in certain families of graphs, and provide evidence for the conjecture that the SOS hierarchy is optimal for testing independence in some regimes. Overall, this work provides a thorough view of the power and limitations of the degree-4 SOS hierarchy for exact inference
This paper presents a novel approach for high-resolution segmentation of tooth root fuzzy edges based on polynomial curve fitting with landmark detection. The proposed method aims to accurately separate the tooth roots from the surrounding dental tissues in digital dental images. Existing methods for tooth root segmentation are often limited by their reliance on hand-crafted features, low accuracy, and high computational complexity. In contrast, our approach leverages the power of polynomial curve fitting to accurately model the fuzzy edges of tooth roots and detect landmarks that define the boundary between the tooth roots and the surrounding tissues. The proposed method consists of three stages: 1) preprocessing, 2) polynomial curve fitting, and 3) landmark detection. In the preprocessing stage, the input image is filtered to enhance the contrast and remove noise. In the polynomial curve fitting stage, a cubic polynomial curve is fitted to the fuzzy edge of the tooth root using a non-linear least squares optimization algorithm. The curve is then used to separate the tooth root from the surrounding tissues. Finally, landmarks are detected by identifying the points on the curve that correspond to the maximum gradient values. The proposed method was evaluated on a dataset of 20 digital dental images using a leave-one-out cross-validation scheme. The results show that our method outperforms existing methods in terms of accuracy, efficiency, and robustness. The mean absolute error was found to be 0.15 mm, and the processing time was less than 30 seconds per image. The proposed method has important implications
In this paper, we present a novel approach to active contours, called deep active contours using locally controlled distance vector flow (DAC-LCDVF). Active contours, also known as snakes or active contours, are a popular method for image segmentation and object recognition. They are based on the idea of evolving a contour to minimize an energy functional that incorporates information about the image and the desired boundary. However, traditional active contour methods can be limited by their reliance on hand-crafted features and their inability to handle complex, high-dimensional data. Our proposed method, DAC-LCDVF, addresses these limitations by combining the power of deep learning with the flexibility of active contours. We use a deep neural network to learn a representation of the image that captures its relevant features, and then use this representation to define a locally controlled distance vector flow (LCDVF) that drives the evolution of the contour. The LCDVF is a generalization of the traditional active contour energy functional, and allows for more flexible and efficient optimization of the contour. We demonstrate the effectiveness of DAC-LCDVF on several challenging image segmentation tasks, including segmenting medical images and object recognition in natural images. Our results show that DAC-LCDVF outperforms traditional active contour methods and other state-of-the-art segmentation techniques. We also provide a thorough analysis of the contributions of the different components of our method, and show that the combination of deep learning and LCDVF is essential for achieving high-quality segmentation results. In this paper, we present a novel approach to active contour models using locally controlled distance vector flow. Traditional active contour models rely on global optimization techniques that can be computationally expensive and may not effectively capture complex image structures. Our proposed method utilizes a locally controlled distance vector flow to drive the evolution of the contour, allowing for efficient and flexible segmentation of images with varying levels of complexity. We propose a new energy functional that incorporates both image information and user interaction, enabling real-time feedback and control over the segmentation process. We further validate our approach by applying it to real-world scenarios such as tumor segmentation in magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) scans. Additionally, the proposed method is able to handle variations in image quality, artifacts, and resolution, and provides accurate segmentation even when the cochlea is not fully visible. In this paper, we propose a novel approach to active contours and image segmentation, which we term the generalized asymmetric dual-front model. This model builds upon the classic active contour model, but incorporates several key advancements that enable it to handle a wider range of image features and produce more accurate segmentation results. The generalized asymmetric dual-front model is based on a generalized form of the active contour energy functional, which includes both image intensity and texture information. The energy functional is minimized using a novel optimization algorithm that combines gradient descent and level set methods. This allows the model to efficiently handle large images and produce high-quality segmentation results. One of the key innovations of the generalized asymmetric dual-front model is the use of an asymmetric dual-front strategy. This involves using two separate fronts, one for the object boundary and one for the background, which are updated alternately. This approach allows the model to more effectively capture the object boundary and reduce the risk of getting stuck in local minima. We demonstrate the effectiveness of the generalized asymmetric dual-front model through experiments on a variety of images, including those with complex objects and textures. The results show that the model produces accurate segmentation results and outperforms several state-of-the-art active contour and image segmentation methods. Overall, the generalized asymmetric dual-front model represents a significant advancement in the field of active contours and image segmentation. Its ability to handle complex images and produce accurate segmentation results makes it a valuable tool
This paper proposes a novel approach to active contour models and image segmentation, entitled the generalized asymmetric dual-front model (GADM). Building on previous work in the field, the GADM extends the classic active contour model by introducing an additional front that moves iteratively towards the object boundary, while simultaneously updating the shape prior. This results in improved efficiency and accuracy, particularly when dealing with complex objects or noisy images. The key innovation of the GADM is its ability to handle various types of object boundaries, including sharp corners, circular arcs, and irregular shapes. By incorporating anisotropic diffusion and a modified gradient flow, the model can effectively capture object boundaries that would be difficult or impossible to detect using traditional methods. The GADM also includes a new stopping criterion, based on the concept of "quasi-convexity," which ensures that the evolution of the contour stops when it has converged to a stable solution. Experiments conducted on several benchmark datasets demonstrate the superior performance of the GADM compared to existing methods, both in terms of computational speed and segmentation accuracy. Additionally, the paper presents theoretical analysis guaranteeing the existence and uniqueness of the solution, as well as convergence properties of the proposed algorithm. Overall, the GADM represents a significant advancement in the field of active contour models and image segmentation, offering a powerful tool for a wide range of applications such medical imaging, computer vision, and graphics.
In this paper, we present a novel approach to real-time semantic segmentation using a revised version of the BiseNet architecture. Our proposed method, called Rethinking BiSeNet, leverages the strengths of the original BiSeNet model while addressing its limitations, particularly in terms of computational efficiency and accuracy. BiSeNet is a popular deep learning architecture for semantic segmentation that uses a combination of binary and multi-class classification to improve performance. However, its computationally intensive nature has limited its use in real-world applications where speed and efficiency are crucial. Our Rethinking BiSeNet approach significantly improves the efficiency of the original model while maintaining its accuracy. We achieve this through a combination of architectural modifications, including depthwise separable convolutions, channel shuffle operations, and a novel feature pyramid network. These modifications allow us to reduce the computational complexity of the model while preserving its ability to capture subtle contextual information. We evaluate our Rethinking BiSeNet model on several benchmark datasets, including Cityscapes, PASCAL VOC, and CamVid. Our results show that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance on these datasets while providing a significant speedup compared to the original BiSeNet model. Specifically, we achieve an improvement of up to 30% in inference speed without sacrificing accuracy. Our work demonstrates the potential of Rethinking BiSeNet for real-time semantic segmentation applications, such as autonomous driving, robotics, and video surveillance. We believe that our approach provides a valuable contribution to the field of computer vision and deep learning, and we hope to inspire further research in this area.

The original BiSeNet was designed as a two-stage framework consisting of a base network followed by a bifurcated branch. The base network generates coarse features, which are then passed through the bifurcated branch to produce fine-grained features. While effective, this approach suffers from several limitations. Firstly, the separate branches result in increased computational complexity, leading to slower processing times. Secondly, the disjoint nature of the branches hinders the sharing of information between different scales, resulting in suboptimal feature extraction. To overcome these issues, we propose a novel integration mechanism that combines the strengths of FCNs and RNNs within the BiSeNet architecture. Specifically, we incorporate 1x1 convolutions and gating mechanisms to selectively focus on relevant spatial locations and channel dimensions. In this paper, we present GENESIS-V2, a novel method for inferring unordered object representations without iterative refinement. Previous methods for inferring object representations have relied on iterative refinement techniques, which can be computationally expensive and may not scale well for large datasets. In contrast, GENESIS-V2 uses a single-pass approach that efficiently infers high-quality object representations without the need for iterative refinement. Our approach is based on a deep neural network architecture that integrates a spatial pyramid pooling module with a self-attention module. The spatial pyramid pooling module captures multi-scale contextual information, while the self-attention module refines the object representations by attendedly weighting the importance of different parts. We introduce a novel training strategy that combines adversarial training with a contrastive loss function, which enables the network to learn a robust and discriminative representation space. We evaluate GENESIS-V2 on several benchmark datasets, including ImageNet and CIFAR-10. Our results show that GENESIS-V2 outperforms state-of-the-art methods for object representation inference, achieving state-of-the-art performance on both datasets without using iterative refinement. We also demonstrate the efficiency of GENESIS-V2, showing that it can infer object representations for large images in a matter of seconds. Our work has important implications for a wide range of computer vision tasks, including object recognition, scene understanding, and image generation. By providing a fast and efficient method for inferring high-quality object representations, GENESIS-V2 has the potential to significantly improve the performance of these tasks.

In this paper, we propose a novel approach to ensemble learning for polyp localization in colonoscopy images. Our method combines classifier prediction confidence and comprehensive learning particle swarm optimization (PSO) to improve the accuracy of polyp detection. First, we train multiple deep learning models as base classifiers to predict the presence or absence of polyps in colonoscopy images. We then calculate the prediction confidence of each classifier and use it as a weighting factor to combine their outputs. This ensures that the final prediction is made by combining the predictions from all classifiers while giving more weightage to the ones with higher prediction confidences. Next, we employ PSO to optimize the hyperparameters of the ensemble model. PSO is a population-based optimization algorithm that simulates the social behavior of bird flocking and fish schooling. It searchs for optimal solutions by iteratively updating the positions of particles in a population, which correspond to the hyperparameters of our model. By using comprehensive learning PSO, we are able to simultaneously optimize both the weights and the biases of the ensemble model. We evaluate our approach on a benchmark dataset consisting of 1000 high-resolution color images of colonoscopies. The results show that our proposed ensemble learning strategy significantly outperforms any single classifier, achieving an improvement in detection accuracy of up to 23%. Additionally, the visualization of the learned features reveals meaningful patterns in the data that can help doctors and researchers better understand the characteristics of polyps and their precursors. Our findings have important implications

Background: Pneumothorax is a life-threatening medical condition characterized by the presence of air in the pleural space between the lungs and chest wall. Prompt diagnosis and treatment are crucial to prevent severe complications. While chest radiography is the standard diagnostic tool, accurate segmentation of pneumothorax can be challenging, especially in cases with incomplete or ambiguous findings. Objective: To develop and validate a deep learning model for automated pneumothorax segmentation in chest radiographs, leveraging state-of-the-art image segmentation techniques to enhance diagnostic accuracy and efficiency. Methods: We retrospectively collected a dataset of 1042 chest radiographs, including 367 images with pneumothorax and 675 without. A preprocessing algorithm was applied to augment the dataset and ensure data quality. We implemented a U-Net architecture tailored to our specific task and optimized it using transfer learning. The model's performance was evaluated against manual annotations created by expert radiologists, focusing on precision, recall, Dice similarity coefficient, and dice loss. Results: Our proposed approach achieved an overall segmentation accuracy of 93.8%, outperforming traditional computer-aided detection (CAD) systems. Furthermore, we analyzed the impact of different window settings on the model's output and found that employing appropriate window sizes improved both sensitivity and specificity in pneumothorax detection. Conclusion: Deep learning-based image segmentation demonstrates great potential in accurately identifying and quantifying pneumothorax extent in chest radiographs. Deep learning techniques have shown promising results in image segmentation tasks; however, their performance can be limited by the gap between the training data distribution and real-world scenarios. Moreover, the lack of interpretability in deep models hinders understanding the reasoning behind their predictions. Objective: To address these challenges, we propose a novel framework called FANet (Feedback Attention Network), which incorporates feedback attention mechanisms to improve the accuracy and interpretability of biomedical image segmentation. Methodology: FANet consists of two main components: an encoder network and a feedback attention module. The encoder network follows a U-Net architecture with skip connections, designed to capture multi-scale contextual information. The feedback attention module is applied on top of the encoder network, consisting of multiple attention layers that focus on different aspects of the input image. Each attention layer computes spatial attention maps to highlight relevant regions, allowing the model to refine its predictions based on the feedback received from previously generated outputs. We implement a self-gated mechanism that adaptively weights the importance of the attention signals, enabling the model to balance between local and global dependencies. Additionally, we introduce a routing strategy that directs the information flow across different attention layers, facilitating better feature representation and exchange. Few-shot segmentation is a challenging task in computer vision that involves segmenting novel objects or classes with only a few labeled examples. Most existing segmentation models rely on supervised learning, which requires a large amount of labeled data to achieve good performance. In this paper, we propose a novel approach called self-guided and cross-guided learning, which enables few-shot segmentation using both self-supervised and cross-supervised learning. Our approach consists of two main components: a self-guided learning module and a cross-guided learning module. The self-guided learning module uses a self-supervised loss function to train the segmentation model on unlabeled data, while the cross-guided learning module uses a cross-supervised loss function to fine-tune the model on labeled data from other related tasks or domains. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach on several benchmark datasets, including PASCAL VOC and Cityscapes. Experiments show that our self-guided and cross-guided learning approach outperforms traditional supervised learning methods, and achieves state-of-the-art performance on few-shot segmentation tasks. We also perform ablation studies to analyze the contributions of different components of our approach, and show that both self-guided and cross-guided learning are important for achieving good performance. Our work has important implications for real-world applications where labeled data is scarce or difficult to obtain. By leveraging large amounts of unlabeled data and related tasks or domains, our approach can learn to segment novel objects or classes with only a few labeled examples, which can significantly reduce the need for manual annotation and improve the efficiency of segmentation model development.

We apply this technique to the task of medical image segmentation, where the goal is to separate the image into its constituent parts or structures. Our approach consists of two main components: (1) a contrastive registration model that aligns images, and (2) a segmentation model that separates the aligned images into their constituent parts. We evaluate our approach on several datasets of medical images, including MRI scans, CT scans, and ultrasound images. Existing methods for federated domain generalization rely on domain-invariant feature learning, which can be limited by the quality of the domain augmentations used. In contrast, FedDG uses a different approach based on episodic learning, which has been shown to be effective in domain adaptation. In FedDG, we first transform the input images into a continuous frequency space using a Fourier transform. We then divide the frequency space into multiple sub-bands, and each client learns a separate model to predict the segmentation mask for each sub-band. The sub-bands are chosen such that they capture the most important frequency information for segmentation. During training, each client updates its local model using its own data, and the updates are combined across clients using a federated averaging method. In addition, we introduce a novel episodic learning strategy that encourages the clients to learn domain-invariant features. Specifically, we create episodes of segmentation tasks, where each episode consists of a random subset of images from multiple domains. The clients are trained to predict the segmentation masks for each episode, and the episodes are designed to gradually increase in difficulty. This paper proposes a novel approach to collaborative sparse regression, which is a technique used to separate the mixed pixel signals in hyperspectral imaging. The proposed method leverages the spatial correlation between pixels and exploits the idea of spatially correlated supports to improve the accuracy and efficiency of the separation process. The proposed method, called Collaborative Sparse Regression using Spatially Correlated Supports (CSR-SCS), combines the strengths of collaborative filtering and compressive sensing to achieve better performance in hyperspectral unmixing. In contrast to traditional methods that rely on independent component analysis or non-negative matrix factorization, CSR-SCS models the mixed pixel signals as a linear combination of basis functions that are shared across neighboring pixels. By incorporating spatial information into the model, CSR-SCS can reduce the dimensionality of the data and improve the identifiability of the sources. The authors evaluate the performance of CSR-SCS using several experiments with real hyperspectral datasets. The results show that CSR-SCS outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of both accuracy and computational efficiency. Specifically, CSR-SCS achieves higher source purity and identification rate compared to traditional methods, while also reducing the computational cost by orders of magnitude. The key contribution of this paper lies in its introduction of spatially correlated supports to collaborative sparse regression, which enables more accurate and efficient hyperspectral unmixing. The proposed method has important implications for a wide range of applications, including environmental monitoring, agricultural management, and military surveillance, where hyperspectral imaging plays an increasingly important role.

In this paper, we present a van der Waals density-functional theory (vdW-DFT) study of bulk solids with body-centered cubic (BCC), face-centered cubic (FCC), and diamond structures. We investigate the structural, electronic, and thermodynamic properties of these materials using vdW-DFT, which includes non-local correlation effects that are important for accurately describing van der Waals interactions between atoms in solids. In this paper, we investigate the performance of van der Waals density-functional theory (vdW-DFT) in describing the structural and thermodynamic properties of bulk solids with body-centered cubic (BCC), face-centered cubic (FCC), and diamond structures. We focus on materials that are relevant to technological applications and are challenging to study using traditional quantum mechanical methods due to their large unit cells and complex structures. Using a range of vdW-DFT functionals, we calculate the lattice parameters, cohesive energies, and phonon spectra of these materials, and compare our results to experimental data and other theoretical methods. We find that vdW-DFT provides a good description of the structural properties of BCC and FCC solids, but underestimates the cohesive energies and overestimates the lattice parameters of diamond. We also investigate the effect of different vdW-DFT functionals on the calculated properties and find that the choice of functional has a significant impact on the results. In particular, we show that the optB88-vdW functional, which includes non-local correlation effects, provides the best overall performance for the materials studied. Our results demonstrate the potential of vdW-DFT for studying the properties of complex bulk solids, while also highlighting the need for further development of functionals that can accurately capture the strong electron correlation and van der Waals interactions present in these materials. Our findings have important implications for the design and optimization of materials for technological applications, such as energy storage and electronics.
In this paper, we introduce and study weighted Sobolev spaces on metric measure spaces. These spaces are generalizations of the classical Sobolev spaces, which are commonly used in analysis and partial differential equations, to the setting of metric measure spaces. The weights allow us to take into account the geometry and topology of the space, as well as any singularities that may be present. We first define the weighted Sobolev norms and show that they satisfy the desired properties, such as being non-negative, symmetric, and satisfied the triangle inequality. We then prove that these norms are equivalent to the classical Sobolev norms on smooth domains, but provide a more flexible framework for studying functions on metric measure spaces with singularities or irregular boundaries. Next, we derive a Poincaré inequality, which is a fundamental tool in analysis, and its weighted version, that allows us to estimate the weighted $L^p$ norm of a function by its gradient. This result has important applications in various fields, including geometric analysis, partial differential equations, and probability theory. Ion transport is a critical process that determines the performance of these devices, yet it remains poorly understood due to its complex nature. Using a combination of theoretical modeling and experimental techniques, we study the dynamics of ions confined in a macroscopic RF linear trap and identify the key mechanisms governing their transport behavior. We demonstrate that ion transport in these systems can be divided into three distinct regimes: (i) diffusion-limited, (ii) electric field-driven, and (iii) magnetron-induced. Our results show that the transition between these regimes is determined by the ratio of the RF frequency to the ion cyclotron frequency, and that the transport rates exhibit non-monotonic dependencies on both the RF amplitude and the magnetic field strength. Furthermore, we find that the presence of a dc electric field can significantly modify the ion transport behavior, leading to enhanced or suppressed transport rates depending on the direction of the field. These findings have important implications for the design and operation of RF linear traps, particularly in the context of scaling up existing designs to larger sizes and improving their overall performance. Our work provides a comprehensive framework for understanding ion transport in these systems and paves the way for further advancements in the development of RF linear traps for a variety of applications.

These traps are commonly used in various fields such as quantum computing, precision measurement, and spectroscopy. Using a combination of theoretical analysis and experimental measurements, we study the ion transport phenomenon in macroscopic RF linear traps. We first develop a theoretical model that describes the motion of ions in the presence of RF fields and thermal motion. We then experimentalize the ion transport by measuring the time-of-flight of ions in a prototype RF linear trap. Our results show that the ion transport in macroscopic RF linear traps is a complex phenomenon that is influenced by various factors such as the RF field strength, the ion density, and the temperature of the environment. We observe that the ion transport is dominated by the thermal motion of ions at low RF field strengths, while at high RF field strengths, the transport is primarily due to the electric field of the RF wave. We also observe that the ion transport exhibits non-linear behavior with respect to the RF field strength, which can lead to the formation of ion clusters and the creation of ionization waves. These phenomena have important implications for the design and operation of RF linear traps, and can be used to optimize their performance for various applications. This paper addresses a fundamental problem in statistics and machine learning: identifying linear regressions when errors are present in all variables. We propose a new method that can accurately identify linear regressions even when measurement errors are present in both the dependent variable and independent variables. Our approach combines techniques from robust statistics, dimensionality reduction, and non-parametric regression to overcome the challenges posed by error-prone data. Using simulations and real-world examples, we demonstrate the effectiveness of our method compared to existing approaches. We show that our method is more accurate and robust than traditional methods, which often fail when faced with errors in multiple variables. Additionally, we provide theoretical guarantees on the consistency and convergence rate of our estimator under certain regularity conditions. Our results have important implications for a wide range of applications, including econometrics, finance, biostatistics, and engineering. In particular, our method provides a reliable way to estimate linear regressions in situations where data quality is poor or uncertain, such as in the presence of missing values, outliers, or measurement errors. Overall, this paper makes an important contribution to the field of statistical modeling and will be useful for researchers and practitioners working with imperfect data.
Abstract: This paper addresses the problem of identifying linear regressions with errors in all variables, a common scenario in many fields where data is subject to measurement errors. We propose a novel method that combines techniques from robust statistics and machine learning to simultaneously identify the linear relationship and handle errors in all variables. Our approach is based on the Huber-White standard error, which allows for the estimation of robust standard errors and p-values for linear regression models with uncertain data. We also propose a modification of the popular lasso regression algorithm to accommodate the presence of errors in all variables. Through a simulation study, we demonstrate the effectiveness of our method in identifying linear regressions with errors in all variables, and compare its performance to alternative approaches. We also apply our method to a real-world dataset to demonstrate its practical applicability. Our results show that our proposed method outperforms existing methods in terms of accuracy and robustness, especially when the number of observations is small and the measurement errors are large. Additionally, our method is found to be more robust to outliers and heavy-tailed distributions, which are common in many fields. Overall, this paper provides a novel approach for identifying linear regressions with errors in all variables, and demonstrates its effectiveness through simulations and real-world applications. The proposed method has important implications for a wide range of fields, including finance, economics, engineering, and environmental science, where data is often subject to measurement errors.
In this paper, we investigate the consistency of matter models with asymptotically safe quantum gravity. Asymptotic safety is a theory that proposes that gravity may become a "safe" theory at very high energies, meaning that the theory becomes self-consistent and predictive. We examine the implications of this idea on matter models, which describe the behavior of particles and fields in the universe. We show that, under certain conditions, matter models can be consistent with asymptotically safe quantum gravity. Specifically, we demonstrate that if the matter sector couples to gravity in a way that respects the symmetries of the gravitational sector, then the matter model can be rendered consistent with the principles of asymptotic safety. Our results have important implications for our understanding of the universe at very small distances and high energies, where both quantum mechanics and general relativity are expected to play important roles. They also provide a new perspective on the search for a complete theory of quantum gravity, highlighting the importance of considering the interplay between matter and gravity in the quest for such a theory. Overall, our work represents an important step forward in our understanding of the relationship between matter and gravity at the smallest scales, and provides a promising direction for future research in theoretical physics.

Abstract: We report on the anisotropic magnetic properties of the URhIn$_{5}$ compound, which exhibits a complex crystal structure and unusual magnetic behavior. Our measurements reveal that the magnetization of URhIn$_{5}$ is highly anisotropic, with a pronounced dependence on the orientation of the magnetic field relative to the crystal lattice. We observe that the magnetic susceptibility varies significantly depending on the direction of the applied field, indicating a strong influence of the crystal structure on the magnetic properties. Our results show that URhIn$_{5}$ exhibits a unique combination of ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic behavior, with a transition between these regimes occurring at low temperatures. Below this transition temperature, we observe a significant enhancement of the magnetic anisotropy, which we attribute to the formation of magnetic domains. Further analysis reveals that the magnetic moments within these domains are aligned in a non-collinear fashion, leading to a rich variety of magnetic phases. Our findings provide new insights into the interplay between crystal structure and magnetic properties in actinide compounds, and highlight the potential of URhIn$_{5}$ as a promising candidate for further study in the search for novel magnetic phenomena.
In this study, we investigate the superconducting properties of niobium (Nb) thin films in the context of BiOCl/Nb heterostructures. We also find that the thickness of the Nb layer plays a critical role in determining the superconducting properties of the heterostructure, with thicker Nb layers resulting in higher critical temperatures. Furthermore, we explore the effect of doping the Nb layers with foreign atoms, such as carbon or nitrogen, on the superconducting properties of the heterostructure. Our results show that doping can significantly enhance the critical temperature of the Nb layers, with some samples displaying superconductivity above 20 K. The observed superconductivity in these heterostructures is attributed to the formation of a two-dimensional electron gas at the interface between the Nb and BiOCl layers. This electron gas is found to be highly mobile and interacts strongly with the substrate, leading to the observed superconducting behavior. Our findings have important implications for the development of high-temperature superconductors based on transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) and their heterostructures. Abstract: In this study, we investigate the superconductivity of niobium (Nb) thin films in heterostructures with bismuth oxychloride (BiOCl) substrates. By synthesizing high-quality BiOCl/Nb heterostructures using molecular beam epitaxy, we observe significant enhancement of superconductivity in the Nb layers. The critical temperature (Tc) of the Nb films in the heterostructures is found to be higher than that of pure Nb films, indicating the presence of a proximity effect. We observe a strong dependence of Tc on the thickness of the BiOCl layer, with a maximum Tc of 17 K achieved for a BiOCl thickness of 8 unit cells. Our results suggest that the BiOCl/Nb heterostructure can be a promising candidate for superconducting devices, and the observed proximity effect may be attributed to the strong electron-phonon coupling and the suppression of charge carrier scattering by the BiOCl substrate. The present study provides new insights into the superconducting properties of Nb-based heterostructures, and may pave the way for the development of novel superconducting materials and devices.
In this paper, we investigate the Ott-Antonsen attractiveness of parameter-dependent oscillatory networks, which are networks of coupled oscillators with parameters that vary periodically in time. We show that the Ott-Antonsen attractiveness of these networks can be used to predict the emergence of synchronized behavior in the network. We first derive a set of equations for the dynamics of the network and demonstrate that the Ott-Antonsen attractiveness can be computed using a set of algebraic equations. We then perform numerical simulations to validate our analytical results and explore the relationship between the Ott-Antonsen attractiveness and the emergence of synchronization in the network. Our results show that the Ott-Antonsen attractiveness is a useful tool for predicting the onset of synchronization in parameter-dependent oscillatory networks. We find that the attractiveness increases as the parameters become more similar across nodes, indicating that synchronization is more likely to occur when the nodes are more homogeneous. Additionally, we find that the attractiveness decreases as the frequency of the external driving signal increases, suggesting that higher frequencies can suppress synchronization. These findings have important implications for understanding the behavior of complex systems, such as biological rhythms, social networks, and power grids. By analyzing the Ott-Antonsen attractiveness of these systems, we can gain insights into their stability and synchronizability, which can inform strategies for controlling and optimizing their behavior. Overall, our work provides new tools and insights for studying the collective behavior of parameter-dependent oscillatory networks.

In this paper, we investigate the concept of almost hypohamiltonian graphs, which are graphs that do not contain a Hamiltonian cycle but have a minimum degree threshold that is close to the maximum possible value. We prove that such graphs exhibit a number of interesting properties and provide a characterization of almost hypohamiltonian graphs in terms of their degree sequence. We show that every almost hypohamiltonian graph has a subgraph that is either a clique or an independent set, and that the complement of an almost hypohamiltonian graph is also almost hypohamiltonian. We also establish bounds on the number of edges and vertices that can be removed from an almost hypohamiltonian graph while preserving its property. Furthermore, we study the algorithmic aspects of almost hypohamiltonian graphs and propose efficient algorithms for finding a maximum-size induced subgraph that is either a clique or an independent set, as well as for testing whether a given graph is almost hypohamiltonian. Our results contribute to a deeper understanding of the structural properties of almost hypohamiltonian graphs and have implications for various applications, including social network analysis, scheduling, and combinatorial optimization.
